</div></section></section><section aria-label="Article content"><section class="body main-article-body"><section class="abstract" id="Abs1"><h2>Abstract</h2>
<section id="sec1"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Background</h3>
<p>The <em>in silico</em> fragmenter MetFrag, launched in 2010, was one of the first approaches combining compound database searching and fragmentation prediction for small molecule identification from tandem mass spectrometry data. Since then many new approaches have evolved, as has MetFrag itself. This article details the latest developments to MetFrag and its use in small molecule identification since the original publication.</p></section><section id="sec2"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Results</h3>
<p>MetFrag has gone through algorithmic and scoring refinements. New features include the retrieval of reference, data source and patent information via ChemSpider and PubChem web services, as well as InChIKey filtering to reduce candidate redundancy due to stereoisomerism. Candidates can be filtered or scored differently based on criteria like occurence of certain elements and/or substructures prior to fragmentation, or presence in so-called “suspect lists”. Retention time information can now be calculated either within MetFrag with a sufficient amount of user-provided retention times, or incorporated separately as “user-defined scores” to be included in candidate ranking. The changes to MetFrag were evaluated on the original dataset as well as a dataset of 473 merged high resolution tandem mass spectra (HR-MS/MS) and compared with another open source <em>in silico </em>fragmenter, CFM-ID. Using HR-MS/MS information only, MetFrag2.2 and CFM-ID had 30 and 43 Top 1 ranks, respectively, using PubChem as a database. Including reference and retention information in MetFrag2.2 improved this to 420 and 336 Top 1 ranks with ChemSpider and PubChem (89 and 71 %), respectively, and even up to 343 Top 1 ranks (PubChem) when combining with CFM-ID. The optimal parameters and weights were verified using three additional datasets of 824 merged HR-MS/MS spectra in total. Further examples are given to demonstrate flexibility of the enhanced features.</p></section><section id="sec3"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Conclusions</h3>
<p>In many cases additional information is available from the experimental context to add to small molecule identification, which is especially useful where the mass spectrum alone is not sufficient for candidate selection from a large number of candidates. The results achieved with MetFrag2.2 clearly show the benefit of considering this additional information. The new functions greatly enhance the chance of identification success and have been incorporated into a command line interface in a flexible way designed to be integrated into high throughput workflows. Feedback on the command line version of MetFrag2.2 available at <a href="http://c-ruttkies.github.io/MetFrag/" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">http://c-ruttkies.github.io/MetFrag/</a> is welcome.</p></section><section id="sec4"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Electronic supplementary material</h3>
<p>The online version of this article (doi:10.1186/s13321-016-0115-9) contains supplementary material, which is available to authorized users.</p></section><section id="kwd-group1" lang="en" class="kwd-group"><p><strong>Keywords:</strong> Compound identification, <em>In silico</em> fragmentation, High resolution mass spectrometry, Metabolomics, Structure elucidation</p></section></section><section id="Sec1"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Background</h2>
<p>The identification of unknown small molecules from mass spectral data is one of the most commonly-mentioned bottlenecks in several scientific fields, including metabolomic, forensic, environmental, pharmaceutical and medical sciences. Recent developments to high resolution, accurate mass spectrometry coupled with chromatographic separation has revolutionized high-throughput analysis and opened up whole new ranges of substances that can be detected at ever decreasing detection limits. However, where “peak inventories” are reported, the vast majority of the substances or peaks detected in samples typically remain unidentified [<a href="#CR1" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR1">1</a>–<a href="#CR3" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR3">3</a>]. Although targeted analysis, where a reference standard is available, remains the best way to confirm the identification of a compound, it is no longer possible to have access to reference standards for the 100s–1000s of substances of interest in complex samples. While mass spectral libraries are growing for high accuracy tandem and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq1"><math id="M2" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mtext>MS</mtext><msup><mrow></mrow><mi>n</mi></msup></mrow></math></span> spectra, the coverage is still relatively small compared with the number of compounds that could potentially be present in typical samples [<a href="#CR4" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR4">4</a>, <a href="#CR5" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR5">5</a>]. Thus, for substances without reference standards or not present in the spectral libraries, the challenge of identification still remains. This has spurred activities in computational mass spectrometry, aimed at proposing tentative identifications for the cases where the mass spectrum is not (yet) in a mass spectral library.</p>
<p>The <em>in silico</em> fragmenter MetFrag, launched in 2010, was one of the first approaches to address this niche for accurate tandem mass spectra in a fast, combinatorial manner [<a href="#CR6" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR6">6</a>]. The MetFrag workflow starts by retrieving candidate structures from the compound databases PubChem [<a href="#CR7" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR7">7</a>], ChemSpider [<a href="#CR8" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR8">8</a>] or KEGG [<a href="#CR9" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR9">9</a>, <a href="#CR10" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR10">10</a>], or accepting the upload of a structure data file (SDF) containing candidates. Candidates are then fragmented using a bond dissociation approach and these fragments are compared with the product ions in the measured mass spectrum to determine which candidates best explain the measured data. The candidate scoring is a function of the mass to charge ratio (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em>),  intensity and bond dissociation energy (BDE) of the matched peaks, while a limited number of neutral loss rules (5 in total) account for rearrangements [<a href="#CR6" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR6">6</a>]. Searching PubChem, the original MetFrag (hereafter termed “MetFrag2010” for readability) achieved a median rank of 8 (with an average of 338 candidates per compound) when restricted to a Feb. 2006 version of PubChem, and 31.5 querying PubChem in 2009 (average of 2508 candidates per compound) on a 102 compound dataset from Hill et al. [<a href="#CR11" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR11">11</a>]. As PubChem is now double the size of the 2009 version, the candidate ranking becomes more challenging over time due to the increase in numbers of candidates. Thus, innovations are required to improve performance and efficiency.</p>
<p>Other methods for <em>in silico</em> fragmentation are also available. The commercial software Mass Frontier [<a href="#CR12" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR12">12</a>] uses rule–based fragmentation prediction based on standard reactions, a comprehensive library of over 100,000 fragmentation rules, or both. The approaches of MetFrag and Mass Frontier are complementary and have been used in combination to support structure elucidation [<a href="#CR13" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR13">13</a>, <a href="#CR14" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR14">14</a>], but Mass Frontier does not perform candidate retrieval or scoring by itself. With increasing amounts of data available, machine learning approaches have been used to train models of the fragmentation process. Heinonen et al. [<a href="#CR15" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR15">15</a>] introduced FingerID, which uses a support vector machine to learn the mapping between the mass spectra and molecular fingerprints of the candidates. Allen et al. [<a href="#CR16" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR16">16</a>] use a stochastic, generative Markov model for the fragmentation. Implemented in CFM-ID (competitive fragment modelling), this can be used to assign fragments to spectra to rank the candidates, but also to predict spectra from structures alone. The MAGMa algorithm [<a href="#CR17" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR17">17</a>] includes information from <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq2"><math id="M4" overflow="linebreak"><msup><mtext>MS</mtext><mi>n</mi></msup></math></span> fragmentation data, but also uses the number of references as an additional scoring term. The latest fragmenter, CSI:FingerID combines fragmentation trees and molecular fingerprinting to achieve up to 39 % Top 1 ranks, outperforming all other fragmenters [<a href="#CR18" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR18">18</a>]. The MetFusion [<a href="#CR19" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR19">19</a>] approach takes advantage of the availability of spectral data for some compounds and performs a combined query of both MetFrag and MassBank [<a href="#CR20" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR20">20</a>], such that the scores of candidates with high chemical similarity to high-scoring reference spectra are increased.</p>
<p>Lessons from recent critical assessment of small molecule identification contests (CASMI) [<a href="#CR21" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR21">21</a>, <a href="#CR22" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR22">22</a>], which included many of the above-mentioned algorithms, show that the use of smaller, specific databases greatly improves the chance of obtaining the correct answer ranked highly and that the winners gathered information from many different sources, rather than relying on the <em>in silico </em>fragmentation alone. Furthermore, performing candidate selection by molecular formula can risk losing the correct candidate if the formula prediction is not certain, such that an exact mass search can be more appropriate in cases where more than one formula is possible. Despite the progress achieved for <em>in silico </em>fragmentation approaches, there are still some fundamental limitations to mass spectrometry that mean that candidate ranking cannot be solved by fragment prediction alone. For example, mass spectra that are dominated by one or only a few fragments (e.g. a water loss) that can be explained by most of the candidates simply do not contain enough information to distinguish candidates. Further examples and limitations are discussed extensively in [<a href="#CR4" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR4">4</a>].</p>
<p>The aim of MetFrag2.2 was to incorporate many additional features into the original MetFrag <em>in silico </em>fragmenter, considering all the information presented above. Features to explicitly include or exclude combinations of elements and substructures by either filtering or scoring were added. Suspect screening approaches, growing in popularity in environmental analysis [<a href="#CR1" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR1">1</a>], were also incorporated to allow users to screen large databases (i.e. PubChem and ChemSpider) while being able to check for candidates present in smaller, more specific databases (e.g. KEGG [<a href="#CR9" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR9">9</a>], HMDB [<a href="#CR23" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR23">23</a>], STOFF-IDENT [<a href="#CR24" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR24">24</a>], MassBank [<a href="#CR20" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR20">20</a>] or NORMAN suspects [<a href="#CR25" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR25">25</a>]), enabling users to “flag” potential structures of interest. The number of references, data sources and/or patents for a substance are now accessible via PubChem and/or ChemSpider web services, and a PubChem reference score has already been included in the MAGMa web interface [<a href="#CR26" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR26">26</a>]. A high number of literature references or patent listings may indicate that the substance is of high use and thus more likely to be found in the environment. Similarly, a higher number of scientific articles for a metabolite could indicate that this has been observed in biological samples before. Reference information has been shown to increase identification “success” in many cases, for example [<a href="#CR17" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR17">17</a>, <a href="#CR27" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR27">27</a>, <a href="#CR28" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR28">28</a>], by providing additional information completely independent of the analytical evidence. However, as this information can introduce a bias towards known compounds, this information should be incorporated with caution, depending on the experimental context.</p>
<p>Retention time information is often used for candidate selection in LC/MS. Unlike the retention index (RI) in GC, where the Kovats RI [<a href="#CR29" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR29">29</a>] is quite widely applied, there is not yet an established RI per se for LC/MS despite a high interest. Instead, where a reverse phase column is used for the LC method, the octanol–water partitioning coefficient (log <em>P</em>) and retention times (RT) of substances can be correlated due to the column properties [<a href="#CR30" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR30">30</a>]. The log <em>P</em> of the measured standards can be predicted with various software approaches and correlated with the retention times (see e.g. [<a href="#CR31" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR31">31</a>] for an overview on different methods). This has already been used in candidate selection (e.g. [<a href="#CR13" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR13">13</a>, <a href="#CR32" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR32">32</a>–<a href="#CR34" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR34">34</a>]), with various log <em>P</em> predictions. The orthogonal information proved useful despite the large errors associated with the predictions (e.g. over 1 log unit or up to several minutes retention time window depending on the LC run length). These are due to uncertainties in log <em>P</em> prediction that are common among different prediction implementations when considering a broad range of substances with different (and many) functional groups and ionization behaviour. As the Chemical Development Kit (CDK [<a href="#CR35" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR35">35</a>, <a href="#CR36" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR36">36</a>]) offers log <em>P</em> calculations, this can be incorporated within MetFrag2.2. Alternative approaches with log <em>D</em>, accounting for ionization, or those requiring more extensive calculations (e.g. [<a href="#CR37" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR37">37</a>–<a href="#CR39" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR39">39</a>]) can be included via a user-defined score, described further below.</p>
<p>This article details the developments and improvements that have been made to MetFrag since the original publication, including a detailed evaluation on several datasets and specific examples to demonstrate the use of MetFrag2.2 in small molecule identification.</p></section><section id="Sec2"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Implementation</h2>
<section id="Sec3"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">MetFrag architecture</h3>
<p>MetFrag2.2 is written in Java and uses the CDK [<a href="#CR35" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR35">35</a>] to read, write and process chemical structures. To start, candidates are selected from a compound database based on the neutral monoisotopic precursor mass and a given relative mass deviation (e.g. 229.1089 ± 5 ppm), the neutral molecular formula of the precursor or a set of database-dependent compound accession numbers. Currently, the online databases KEGG [<a href="#CR9" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR9">9</a>, <a href="#CR10" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR10">10</a>], PubChem [<a href="#CR7" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR7">7</a>] or ChemSpider [<a href="#CR8" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR8">8</a>] can be used with MetFrag2.2, as well as offline databases in the form of a structure data file (SDF) or, new to MetFrag2.2, a CSV file that contains structures in the form of InChIs [<a href="#CR40" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR40">40</a>] together with their identifiers and other properties. Furthermore, MetFrag2.2 is able to query local compound database systems in MySQL or PostgreSQL, as performed in [<a href="#CR41" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR41">41</a>].</p>
<p>MetFrag2010 considered the ion species [M + H]<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq4"><math id="M6" overflow="linebreak"><msup><mrow></mrow><mo>+</mo></msup></math></span>, [M]<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq5"><math id="M8" overflow="linebreak"><msup><mrow></mrow><mo>+</mo></msup></math></span>, [M]<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq6"><math id="M10" overflow="linebreak"><msup><mrow></mrow><mo>-</mo></msup></math></span> and [M − H]<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq8"><math id="M12" overflow="linebreak"><msup><mrow></mrow><mo>-</mo></msup></math></span> during candidate retrieval and fragment generation. While the web interface contained an adduct mass adjustment feature, the presence of adducts was not considered in the fragments. MetFrag2.2 can also handle adducts also appearing in the product ions associated with [M + Na]<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq9"><math id="M14" overflow="linebreak"><msup><mrow></mrow><mo>+</mo></msup></math></span>, [M + K]<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq10"><math id="M16" overflow="linebreak"><msup><mrow></mrow><mo>+</mo></msup></math></span>, [M + NH<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq11"><math id="M18" overflow="linebreak"><msub><mrow></mrow><mn>4</mn></msub></math></span>]<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq12"><math id="M20" overflow="linebreak"><msup><mrow></mrow><mo>+</mo></msup></math></span> for positive ionization and [M + Cl]<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq13"><math id="M22" overflow="linebreak"><msup><mrow></mrow><mo>-</mo></msup></math></span>, [M + HCOO]<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq14"><math id="M24" overflow="linebreak"><msup><mrow></mrow><mo>-</mo></msup></math></span> and [M + CH<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq15"><math id="M26" overflow="linebreak"><msub><mrow></mrow><mn>3</mn></msub></math></span>COO]<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq16"><math id="M28" overflow="linebreak"><msup><mrow></mrow><mo>-</mo></msup></math></span> for negative ionization. As the candidate retrieval is performed on neutral molecules, the precursor adduct type must still be known beforehand; for high-throughput workflows this information is intended to come from the workflow output.</p>
<p>Additive relative and absolute mass deviation values are used to perform the MS/MS peak matching and can be adjusted according to the instrument type used for MS/MS spectra acquisition. The number of fragmentation steps performed by MetFrag2.2 can be limited by setting the tree depth (default is 2).</p>
<p>The overall score of a given candidate is calculated as shown in Eq. <a href="#Equ1" class="usa-link">1</a>.</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="Equ1"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="M30" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mtable columnspacing="0.5ex"><mtr><mtd columnalign="right"><msub><mi>S</mi><msub><mi>C</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">Final</mi></msub></msub></mtd><mtd columnalign="left"><mrow><mo>=</mo><mrow></mrow><msub><mi mathvariant="italic">ω</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">Frag</mi></msub><mo>·</mo><msub><mi>S</mi><msub><mi>C</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">Frag</mi></msub></msub><mo>+</mo><msub><mi mathvariant="italic">ω</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">RT</mi></msub><mo>·</mo><msub><mi>S</mi><msub><mi>C</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">RT</mi></msub></msub><mo>+</mo><msub><mi mathvariant="italic">ω</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">Refs</mi></msub><mo>·</mo><msub><mi>S</mi><msub><mi>C</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">Refs</mi></msub></msub><mo>+</mo><msub><mi mathvariant="italic">ω</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">Incl</mi></msub><mo>·</mo><msub><mi>S</mi><msub><mi>C</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">Incl</mi></msub></msub></mrow></mtd></mtr><mtr><mtd columnalign="right"><mrow></mrow></mtd><mtd columnalign="left"><mrow><mspace width="1em"></mspace><mo>+</mo><mspace width="0.166667em"></mspace><msub><mi mathvariant="italic">ω</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">Excl</mi></msub><mo>·</mo><msub><mi>S</mi><msub><mi>C</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">Excl</mi></msub></msub><mo>+</mo><msub><mi mathvariant="italic">ω</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">Suspects</mi></msub><mo>·</mo><msub><mi>S</mi><msub><mi>C</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">Suspects</mi></msub></msub><mo>+</mo><mo>⋯</mo><mo>+</mo><msub><mi mathvariant="italic">ω</mi><mi>n</mi></msub><mo>·</mo><msub><mi>S</mi><msub><mi>C</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">n</mi></msub></msub></mrow></mtd></mtr></mtable></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">1</td>
</tr></table>
<p>The final candidate score <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq21"><math id="M32" overflow="linebreak"><msub><mi>S</mi><msub><mi>C</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">Final</mi></msub></msub></math></span> is the weighted sum of all single scoring terms used, where the weights given by <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq22"><math id="M34" overflow="linebreak"><msub><mi mathvariant="italic">ω</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></math></span> specify the contribution of each term. All <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq23"><math id="M36" overflow="linebreak"><msub><mi>S</mi><mi>C</mi></msub></math></span> scoring terms used to calculate <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq24"><math id="M38" overflow="linebreak"><msub><mi>S</mi><msub><mi>C</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">Final</mi></msub></msub></math></span> are normalized to the maximum value within the candidate result list for a given MS/MS input. The calculation of individual scoring terms are detailed in the subsections below; all terms besides <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq25"><math id="M40" overflow="linebreak"><msub><mi>S</mi><msub><mi>C</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">Frag</mi></msub></msub></math></span> are new to MetFrag2.2.</p>
<p>A variety of output options are available. Output SDFs contain all compounds with a structure connection table and all additional information stored in property fields. For the CSV and XLS format, the structures are encoded by SMILES [<a href="#CR42" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR42">42</a>] and InChI codes, while an extended XLS option is available that includes images of the compounds and/or fragments. In all cases the compounds are sorted by the calculated score by default.</p></section><section id="Sec4"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">
<em>In silico</em> fragmentation refinements</h3>
<p>The <em>in silico </em>fragmentation part of MetFrag2.2 has undergone extensive algorithmic and scoring refinements. The fragmentation algorithm still uses a top-down approach, starting with an entire molecular graph and removing each bond successively. However, the generated fragments are now stored more efficiently by using only the indexes of removed bonds and atoms, similar to the MAGMa approach [<a href="#CR43" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR43">43</a>]. This not only increases processing speed and decreases memory usage, but still allows the fast calculation of the masses and molecular formulas of each fragment. This makes it possible to process MS/MS spectra with higher tree depths to generate reliable fragments for molecules with complex ring structures with lower CPU and memory requirements. As a result, fragment filters such as the molecular formula duplicate filter used in MetFrag2010 to decrease the number of generated structures were no longer required, their removal reduces the risk of missing a potentially correct fragment. The calculation of the fragmentation score, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq36"><math id="M42" overflow="linebreak"><msub><mi>S</mi><msub><mi>C</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">Frag</mi></msub></msub></math></span>, modified from the score given in [<a href="#CR6" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR6">6</a>], is shown in Eq. <a href="#Equ2" class="usa-link">2</a> for a given candidate C:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="Equ2"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="M44" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>S</mi><msub><mi>C</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">Frag</mi></msub></msub><mo>=</mo><munder><mo movablelimits="false">∑</mo><mrow><mi>p</mi><mo>∈</mo><mi>P</mi></mrow></munder><mfrac><mrow><msup><mrow><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">RelMass</mi><mi>p</mi></msub></mrow><mi mathvariant="italic">α</mi></msup><mo>·</mo><msup><mrow><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">RelInt</mi><mi>p</mi></msub></mrow><mi mathvariant="italic">β</mi></msup></mrow><msup><mfenced close=")" open="(" separators=""><msub><mo>∑</mo><mrow><mi>b</mi><mo>∈</mo><msub><mi>B</mi><mi>f</mi></msub></mrow></msub><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">BDE</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">b</mi></msub></mfenced><mi mathvariant="italic">γ</mi></msup></mfrac></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">2</td>
</tr></table>
<p>For each peak <em>p</em> matching a generated fragment, the relative mass <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq37"><math id="M46" overflow="linebreak"><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">RelMass</mi><mi>p</mi></msub></math></span> and intensity <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq38"><math id="M48" overflow="linebreak"><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">RelInt</mi><mi>p</mi></msub></math></span> as well as the sum of all cleaved bonds <em>b</em> of the fragment <em>f</em> assigned to <em>p</em> are considered. Where more than one fragment could be assigned to <em>p</em>, the fragment with the lowest denominator value is considered. In contrast to Eq. <a href="#Equ2" class="usa-link">2</a>, the MetFrag2010 scoring used the difference between <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq39"><math id="M50" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo stretchy="false">/</mo><mi>m</mi><mi>a</mi><mi>x</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>w</mi><mi>c</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></math></span> and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq40"><math id="M52" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo stretchy="false">/</mo><mi>m</mi><mi>a</mi><mi>x</mi><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>e</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo>·</mo><msub><mi>e</mi><mi>c</mi></msub></mrow></math></span>, which could lead to negative scores if the BDE penalty was large. The weights <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq41"><math id="M54" overflow="linebreak"><mi mathvariant="italic">α</mi></math></span>, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq42"><math id="M56" overflow="linebreak"><mi mathvariant="italic">β</mi></math></span> and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq43"><math id="M58" overflow="linebreak"><mi mathvariant="italic">γ</mi></math></span> were optimized on a smaller subset of spectra from Gerlich and Neumann [<a href="#CR19" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR19">19</a>] that was not used further in this work including merged MassBank IPB (PB) and RIKEN (PR) MS/MS spectra and were set to <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq44"><math id="M60" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mi mathvariant="italic">α</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1.84</mn></mrow></math></span>, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq45"><math id="M62" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mi mathvariant="italic">β</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>0.59</mn></mrow></math></span> and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq46"><math id="M64" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mi mathvariant="italic">γ</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>0.47</mn></mrow></math></span>. Once <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq47"><math id="M66" overflow="linebreak"><msub><mi>S</mi><msub><mi>C</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">Frag</mi></msub></msub></math></span> has been calculated for all candidates within a candidate list, it is normalised so that the highest score is one.</p></section><section id="Sec5"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Compound filters, element and substructure options</h3>
<p>The <em>unconnected compound filter</em> was already implemented in MetFrag2010 to remove salts and other unconnected substances that could not possibly have the correct neutral mass from the candidate list. InChIKey filtering has now been added to reduce candidate redundancy due to stereoisomerism, as stereoisomers inflate candidate numbers but cannot (usually) be distinguished with MS/MS. The InChIKey filtering is performed using the first block, which encodes the molecular skeleton (or connectivity), but not the stereochemistry. While this is generally reasonable, some tautomers may have differing InChIKey first blocks (see e.g. [<a href="#CR40" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR40">40</a>]), such that not all tautomers will be filtered out. The highest-scoring stereoisomers overall with a matching first block are retained.</p>
<p><em>Element restrictions</em> have been added to enhance the specificity of the exact mass search. Three options are available to restrict the elements considered: (a) include <em>only</em> the given elements, (b) the given elements have to be present, but other elements can also be present (as long as they are not explicitly excluded) and (c) exclude certain elements. Options (b) and (c) can be used in combination. These filters can be used for example to incorporate isotope information (e.g. Cl, S) that has been detected in the full scan (MS1) data.</p>
<p><em>Substructure restrictions</em> allow the inclusion and exclusion of certain molecular substructures, encoded in SMARTS [<a href="#CR44" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR44">44</a>]. Each substructure is searched independently, thus overlapping substructures can also be considered. This option is particularly useful for cases where detailed information about a parent substance is known (e.g. transformation product, metabolite elucidation), or complementary substructure information is available from elsewhere (e.g. MS2Analyzer [<a href="#CR45" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR45">45</a>] or other MS classifiers [<a href="#CR13" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR13">13</a>]). Candidates containing certain substructures can either be included and/or excluded prior to fragmentation, or scored differently. To calculate a score, the number of matches in the inclusion or exclusion list containing <em>n</em> substructures are added per candidate as given in Eq. <a href="#Equ3" class="usa-link">3</a> (where <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq48"><math id="M68" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>M</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>,</mo></mrow></math></span> if substructure <em>i</em> matches candidate <em>C</em> from the given candidate list <em>L</em> or 0 otherwise):</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="Equ3"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="M70" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>N</mi><msub><mi>C</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">Match</mi></msub></msub><mo>=</mo><mo>∑</mo><msub><mi>M</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo>+</mo><msub><mi>M</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><mo>+</mo><mo>⋯</mo><mo>+</mo><msub><mi>M</mi><mi>n</mi></msub><mo>;</mo><mspace width="1em"></mspace><msub><mi>M</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo>∈</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">{</mo><mn>0</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>1</mn><mo stretchy="false">}</mo></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">3</td>
</tr></table>
<p>The inclusion (<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq49"><math id="M72" overflow="linebreak"><msub><mi>S</mi><msub><mi>C</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">Incl</mi></msub></msub></math></span>) and/or exclusion (<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq50"><math id="M74" overflow="linebreak"><msub><mi>S</mi><msub><mi>C</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">Excl</mi></msub></msub></math></span>) score(s) per candidate are then calcualted as shown in Eq. <a href="#Equ4" class="usa-link">4</a>:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="Equ4"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="M76" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>S</mi><msub><mi>C</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">Incl</mi></msub></msub><mo>=</mo><mfrac><msub><mi>N</mi><msub><mi>C</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">Match</mi></msub></msub><mrow><mi>m</mi><mi>a</mi><msub><mi>x</mi><mrow><msup><mi>C</mi><mo>′</mo></msup><mo>∈</mo><mi>L</mi></mrow></msub><mfenced close=")" open="(" separators=""><msub><mi>N</mi><msub><mrow><msup><mi>C</mi><mo>′</mo></msup></mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">Match</mi></msub></msub></mfenced></mrow></mfrac><mo>;</mo><mspace width="1em"></mspace><msub><mi>S</mi><msub><mi>C</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">Excl</mi></msub></msub><mo>=</mo><mfrac><mrow><mi>n</mi><mo>-</mo><msub><mi>N</mi><msub><mi>C</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">Match</mi></msub></msub></mrow><mrow><mi>m</mi><mi>a</mi><msub><mi>x</mi><mrow><msup><mi>C</mi><mo>′</mo></msup><mo>∈</mo><mi>L</mi></mrow></msub><mfenced close=")" open="(" separators=""><mi>n</mi><mo>-</mo><msub><mi>N</mi><msub><mrow><msup><mi>C</mi><mo>′</mo></msup></mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">Match</mi></msub></msub></mfenced></mrow></mfrac></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">4</td>
</tr></table>
<p>where <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq51"><math id="M78" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mi>m</mi><mi>a</mi><msub><mi>x</mi><mrow><msup><mi>C</mi><mo>′</mo></msup><mo>∈</mo><mi>L</mi></mrow></msub><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>N</mi><msub><mrow><msup><mi>C</mi><mo>′</mo></msup></mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">Match</mi></msub></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow></math></span> is the maximal value of <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq52"><math id="M80" overflow="linebreak"><msub><mi>N</mi><msub><mi>C</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">Match</mi></msub></msub></math></span> within the candidate list and the scores <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq53"><math id="M82" overflow="linebreak"><msub><mi>S</mi><msub><mi>C</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">Incl</mi></msub></msub></math></span> or <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq54"><math id="M84" overflow="linebreak"><msub><mi>S</mi><msub><mi>C</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">Excl</mi></msub></msub></math></span> are set to 0 when <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq55"><math id="M86" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mi>m</mi><mi>a</mi><msub><mi>x</mi><mrow><msup><mi>C</mi><mo>′</mo></msup><mo>∈</mo><mi>L</mi></mrow></msub><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>N</mi><msub><mrow><msup><mi>C</mi><mo>′</mo></msup></mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">Match</mi></msub></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo>=</mo><mn>0</mn></mrow></math></span> or <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq56"><math id="M88" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mi>m</mi><mi>a</mi><msub><mi>x</mi><mrow><msup><mi>C</mi><mo>′</mo></msup><mo>∈</mo><mi>L</mi></mrow></msub><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>n</mi><mo>-</mo><msub><mi>N</mi><msub><mrow><msup><mi>C</mi><mo>′</mo></msup></mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">Match</mi></msub></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo>=</mo><mn>0</mn></mrow></math></span>, respectively.</p></section><section id="Sec6"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Additional substance information</h3>
<section id="Sec7"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">Reference and patent information</h4>
<p>While the reference and patent information is represented by the placeholder term <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq75"><math id="M90" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi mathvariant="italic">ω</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">Refs</mi></msub><mo>·</mo><msub><mi>S</mi><msub><mi>C</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">Refs</mi></msub></msub></mrow></math></span> in Eq. <a href="#Equ1" class="usa-link">1</a>, the score can either be composed of several terms or added as a combined term, as described below.</p>
<p>If the query databases is PubChem, the number of patents (PubChemNumberPatents, PNP) and PubMed references (PubChemPubMedCount, PPC) are retrieved for each candidate via the PubChem PUG REST API [<a href="#CR46" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR46">46</a>]. These values result in the scoring terms <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq76"><math id="M92" overflow="linebreak"><msub><mi>S</mi><msub><mi>C</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">PNP</mi></msub></msub></math></span> and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq77"><math id="M94" overflow="linebreak"><msub><mi>S</mi><msub><mi>C</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">PPC</mi></msub></msub></math></span>, which can be weighted individually, or a combined term with either or both parameters. For the latter, first, a cumulative reference term is calculated as shown in Eq. <a href="#Equ5" class="usa-link">5</a>, before the PubChem combined reference score (<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq78"><math id="M96" overflow="linebreak"><msub><mi>S</mi><msub><mi>C</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">PCR</mi></msub></msub></math></span>) is calculated for candidate <em>C</em> in candidate list <em>L</em> as shown in Eq. <a href="#Equ6" class="usa-link">6</a> for PubChem:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="Equ5"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="M98" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>N</mi><msub><mi>C</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">PCR</mi></msub></msub><mo>=</mo><msub><mi>a</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo>·</mo><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">PNP</mi><mi>C</mi></msub><mo>+</mo><msub><mi>a</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><mo>·</mo><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">PPC</mi><mi>C</mi></msub><mo>,</mo><mspace width="1em"></mspace><msub><mi>a</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>a</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><mo>∈</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">{</mo><mn>0</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>1</mn><mo stretchy="false">}</mo></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">5</td>
</tr></table>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="Equ6"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="M100" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>S</mi><msub><mi>C</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">PCR</mi></msub></msub><mo>=</mo><mfrac><msub><mi>N</mi><msub><mi>C</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">PCR</mi></msub></msub><mrow><mi>m</mi><mi>a</mi><msub><mi>x</mi><mrow><msup><mi>C</mi><mo>′</mo></msup><mo>∈</mo><mi>L</mi></mrow></msub><msub><mi>N</mi><msubsup><mi>C</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">PCR</mi><mo>′</mo></msubsup></msub></mrow></mfrac></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">6</td>
</tr></table>
<p>For ChemSpider, five values with reference information can be retrieved using the ChemSpider web services [<a href="#CR47" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR47">47</a>]), including the number of data sources (ChemSpiderDataSourceCount, CDC), references (ChemspiderReferenceCount, CRC), PubMed references (ChemSpiderPubMedCount, CPC), Royal Society for Chemistry (RSC) references (ChemSpiderRSCCount, CRSC) and external references (ChemSpiderExternalReferenceCount, CERC). Any combination of these reference sources can be used and weighted individually, yielding the score terms <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq79"><math id="M102" overflow="linebreak"><msub><mi>S</mi><msub><mi>C</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">CDC</mi></msub></msub></math></span>, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq80"><math id="M104" overflow="linebreak"><msub><mi>S</mi><msub><mi>C</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">CRC</mi></msub></msub></math></span>, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq81"><math id="M106" overflow="linebreak"><msub><mi>S</mi><msub><mi>C</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">CPC</mi></msub></msub></math></span>, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq82"><math id="M108" overflow="linebreak"><msub><mi>S</mi><msub><mi>C</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">CRSC</mi></msub></msub></math></span> and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq83"><math id="M110" overflow="linebreak"><msub><mi>S</mi><msub><mi>C</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">CERC</mi></msub></msub></math></span>. Alternatively, the ChemSpider Combined Reference Scoring term (<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq84"><math id="M112" overflow="linebreak"><msub><mi>S</mi><msub><mi>C</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">CCR</mi></msub></msub></math></span>) can be calculated, as shown below in Eqs. <a href="#Equ7" class="usa-link">7</a> and <a href="#Equ8" class="usa-link">8</a>:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="Equ7"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="M114" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mtable columnspacing="0.5ex"><mtr><mtd columnalign="right"><msub><mi>N</mi><msub><mi>C</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">CCR</mi></msub></msub></mtd><mtd columnalign="left"><mrow><mo>=</mo><msub><mi>b</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo>·</mo><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">CRC</mi><mi>C</mi></msub><mo>+</mo><msub><mi>b</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><mo>·</mo><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">CERC</mi><mi>C</mi></msub><mo>+</mo><msub><mi>b</mi><mn>3</mn></msub><mo>·</mo><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">CRSC</mi><mi>C</mi></msub></mrow></mtd></mtr><mtr><mtd columnalign="right"><mrow></mrow></mtd><mtd columnalign="left"><mrow><mspace width="1em"></mspace><mo>+</mo><msub><mi>b</mi><mn>4</mn></msub><mo>·</mo><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">CPC</mi><mi>C</mi></msub><mo>+</mo><msub><mi>b</mi><mn>5</mn></msub><mo>·</mo><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">CDC</mi><mi>C</mi></msub><mspace width="1em"></mspace><msub><mi>b</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>b</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>b</mi><mn>3</mn></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>b</mi><mn>4</mn></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>b</mi><mn>5</mn></msub><mo>∈</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">{</mo><mn>0</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>1</mn><mo stretchy="false">}</mo></mrow></mrow></mtd></mtr></mtable></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">7</td>
</tr></table>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="Equ8"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="M116" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>S</mi><msub><mi>C</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">CCR</mi></msub></msub><mo>=</mo><mfrac><msub><mi>N</mi><msub><mi>C</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">CCR</mi></msub></msub><mrow><mi>m</mi><mi>a</mi><msub><mi>x</mi><mrow><msup><mi>C</mi><mo>′</mo></msup><mo>∈</mo><mi>L</mi></mrow></msub><mspace width="0.166667em"></mspace><msub><mi>N</mi><msubsup><mi>C</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">CCR</mi><mo>′</mo></msubsup></msub></mrow></mfrac></mrow></math></td>

<p>The corresponding command line terms are given in the additional information (see Additional files <a href="#MOESM1" class="usa-link">1</a>, <a href="#MOESM2" class="usa-link">2</a>, <a href="#MOESM3" class="usa-link">3</a>).</p></section><section id="Sec8"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">Suspect lists</h4>
<p>Additional lists of substances (so-called “suspect lists”) can be used to screen for the presence of retrieved candidates in alternative databases. The suspect lists are input as a text file containing InChIKeys (one key per line) for fast screening. The first block of the InChIKey is used to determine matches. Example files are available from [<a href="#CR25" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR25">25</a>]. This “suspect screening” can be used as an inclusion filter (include only those substances that are in the suspect list) or as an additional scoring term for the ranking of the candidates, yielding the term <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq98"><math id="M118" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi mathvariant="italic">ω</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">Suspects</mi></msub><mo>·</mo><msub><mi>S</mi><msub><mi>C</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">Suspects</mi></msub></msub></mrow></math></span> given in Eq. <a href="#Equ1" class="usa-link">1</a>.</p></section></section><section id="Sec9"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Retention time score via log <em>P</em>
</h3>
<p>The retention time (RT) scores offered within MetFrag2.2 are based on the correlation of log <em>P</em> and user-provided RT information. The RTs must be associated with sufficient analytical standards measured under the same conditions as the unknown spectrum (a minimum of ten data points are recommended, depending on the distribution over the chromatographic run). By default, the log <em>P</em> is calculated using the XlogP algorithm in the CDK library [<a href="#CR36" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR36">36</a>, <a href="#CR48" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR48">48</a>, <a href="#CR49" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR49">49</a>]. Alternatively, if PubChem is used as a candidate source, the XLOGP3 value retrieved from PubChem can also be used [<a href="#CR50" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR50">50</a>]. The user-provided RTs and their associated log <em>P</em> values comprise a training dataset to generate a linear model between RT and the log <em>P</em>, shown in Eq. <a href="#Equ9" class="usa-link">9</a>, where <em>a</em> and <em>b</em> are determined using least squares regression:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="Equ9"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="M120" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">log</mi><mspace width="0.166667em"></mspace><msub><mi>P</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">Unknown</mi></msub><mo>=</mo><mi>a</mi><mo>·</mo><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">RT</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">Unknown</mi></msub><mo>+</mo><mi>b</mi></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">9</td>
</tr></table>
<p>This equation is then used to estimate log <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq99"><math id="M122" overflow="linebreak"><msub><mi>P</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">Unknown</mi></msub></math></span>, given the measured RT associated with the unknown spectrum, and compared with log <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq100"><math id="M124" overflow="linebreak"><msub><mi>P</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">C</mi></msub></math></span> calculated for each candidate. It is imperative that the log <em>P</em> calculated for each candidate arises from the same source as the log <em>P</em> used to build the model in Eq. <a href="#Equ9" class="usa-link">9</a>. Lower log <em>P</em> deviations result in a higher score for a candidate; the score is calculated using density functions assuming a normal distribution with <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq101"><math id="M126" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mi mathvariant="italic">σ</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1.5</mn></mrow></math></span> (chosen arbitrarily), as shown in Eq. <a href="#Equ10" class="usa-link">10</a>:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="Equ10"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="M128" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>S</mi><msub><mi>C</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">RT</mi></msub></msub><mo>=</mo><mfrac><mn>1</mn><mrow><mi mathvariant="italic">σ</mi><msqrt><mrow><mn>2</mn><mi mathvariant="italic">π</mi></mrow></msqrt></mrow></mfrac><msup><mi>e</mi><mrow><mrow><mo>-</mo><msup><mfenced close=")" open="(" separators=""><mrow><mrow><mo stretchy="false">|</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">log</mi><mspace width="0.166667em"></mspace></mrow><msub><mi>P</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">Unknown</mi></msub><mo>-</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">log</mi><mspace width="0.166667em"></mspace><msub><mi>P</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">C</mi></msub><mrow><mo stretchy="false">|</mo></mrow></mrow></mfenced><mn>2</mn></msup></mrow><mo stretchy="false">/</mo><mrow><mn>2</mn><msup><mi mathvariant="italic">σ</mi><mn>2</mn></msup></mrow></mrow></msup></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">10</td>
</tr></table>
<p>Alternative log <em>P</em> values that are not available within MetFrag2.2 can also be used to establish a model and calculate a different <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq102"><math id="M130" overflow="linebreak"><msub><mi>S</mi><msub><mi>C</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">RT</mi></msub></msub></math></span> in a two-step approach. First, MetFrag2.2 can be run either with or without one of the built-in models, so that candidates and all other scores can be obtained. The InChIs or SMILES in the output CSV, or structures in the output SDF can then be used by the user to calculate their own log <em>P</em> values. These should be included in the output CSV or SDF using the “UserLogP” tag (or a self-defined alternative) and used as input for MetFrag2.2 with the Local Database option and a RT training file containing retention times and the user log <em>P</em>s with the column header matching the tag in the results file. The values <em>a</em> and <em>b</em> in Eq. <a href="#Equ9" class="usa-link">9</a> are then determined and used to calculate <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq103"><math id="M132" overflow="linebreak"><msub><mi>S</mi><msub><mi>C</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">RT</mi></msub></msub></math></span> for the final scoring. Alternative RT models that do not use log <em>P</em> should be included as a “user-defined score”, as described below.</p></section><section id="Sec10"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">User-defined scoring functions</h3>
<p>The final term in Eq. <a href="#Equ1" class="usa-link">1</a>, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq104"><math id="M134" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi mathvariant="italic">ω</mi><mi>n</mi></msub><mo>·</mo><msub><mi>S</mi><msub><mi>C</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">n</mi></msub></msub></mrow></math></span>, represents the “user-defined scoring function”, which allows users to incorporate any additional information into the final candidate scoring. The MetFrag2.2 output (InChIs, SMILES, SDF) can be used to calculate additional “scores” for the candidates using external methods and these scores can be reimported with the candidates and all other MetFrag2.2 scores in the pipe-separated (|) format for final scoring. The scores and weights are matched from the column headers in the input file and the parameter names added to the score list. The commands are given in a additional table (see Additional files <a href="#MOESM1" class="usa-link">1</a>, <a href="#MOESM2" class="usa-link">2</a>, <a href="#MOESM3" class="usa-link">3</a>), with an example (“terbutylazine and isomers”) below.</p></section></section><section id="Sec11"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Results and discussion</h2>
<p>The changes to MetFrag2.2 were evaluated on several datasets, described in the following. Further examples are given to demonstrate the use of different new features. Unless mentioned otherwise, candidate structures were retrieved from the compound databases PubChem and ChemSpider in June, 2015. If not stated explicitly, the datasets were processed with a relative and absolute fragment mass deviation of 5 ppm and 0.001 Da, respectively. The resulting ranks, if not specified explicitly, correspond to pessimistic ranks, where the worst rank is reported in the case where the correct candidate has the same score as other candidates. Stereoisomers were filtered to keep only the best scored candidate based on the comparison of the first part of the candidates’ InChIKeys. The expected top ranks calculated as in Allen et al. [<a href="#CR16" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR16">16</a>], which handles ties of equally scored candidates in a uniformly random manner, are also given when comparing the two <em>in silico </em>fragmenters. This demonstrates the effect of equally scored candidates on ranking results.</p>
<p>The datasets from Eawag and UFZ used in this publication arose from the measurement of reference standard collections at Eawag and UFZ, which comprise small molecules of environmental relevance such as pharmaceuticals and pesticides with a wide range of physico-chemical properties and functional groups, and also include several transformation products which typically have lower reference counts. All spectra are publicly available in MassBank.</p>
<section id="Sec12"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">
<em>In Silico</em> fragmentation performance</h3>
<section id="Sec13"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">Comparison with MetFrag2010</h4>
<p>The merged spectra from 102 compounds published in Hill et al. [<a href="#CR11" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR11">11</a>], also used in [<a href="#CR6" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR6">6</a>, <a href="#CR19" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR19">19</a>], formed the first evaluation set. The candidate sets from Gerlich and Neumann [<a href="#CR19" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR19">19</a>] were used as input for MetFrag2.2 and processed with consistent settings: relative mass deviation of 10 ppm and absolute mass deviation of 0 Da, i.e. no absolute error, for a direct comparison with MetFrag2010. With MetFrag2.2, the median rank improved from 18.5 to 14.5, while the number of correct ranked candidates in the top 1, 3 and 5 improved from 8 to 9, 20 to 24 and 28 to 34, respectively.</p></section><section id="Sec14"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">Baseline performance on Orbitrap XL Dataset</h4>
<p>A set of 473 LTQ Orbitrap XL spectra resulting from 359 reference standards formed the second dataset. The spectra were measured at several collision energies with both collision-induced ionization (CID) 35 and higher-energy CID (HCD) 15, 30, 45, 60, 75 and 90 normalized units (see [<a href="#CR51" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR51">51</a>] for more details) coupled with liquid chromatography (LC) with a 25 min program on an Xbridge C18 column. The raw files were processed with RMassBank [<a href="#CR51" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR51">51</a>, <a href="#CR52" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR52">52</a>], yielding the “EA” records in MassBank. These spectra were merged using the mzClust_hclust function in xcms [<a href="#CR53" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR53">53</a>] (parameters eppm = <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq106"><math id="M136" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mn>5</mn><mo>×</mo><msup><mn>10</mn><mrow><mo>-</mo><mn>6</mn></mrow></msup></mrow></math></span> and eabs = 0.001 Da) to create peaks with the mean <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> value and highest (relative) intensity and retained where they contained at least one fragment peak other than the precursor. In total 473 spectra (319 [M + H]<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq108"><math id="M138" overflow="linebreak"><msup><mrow></mrow><mo>+</mo></msup></math></span>and 154 [M − H]<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq110"><math id="M140" overflow="linebreak"><msup><mrow></mrow><mo>-</mo></msup></math></span>) were evaluated with MetFrag2010 using ChemSpider, as well as MetFrag2.2 using either PubChem or ChemSpider. The correct molecular formula was used to retrieve candidates. The results, given in Table <a href="#Tab1" class="usa-link">1</a>, show the clear improvement between MetFrag2010 (73 Top 1 ranks with ChemSpider) and MetFrag2.2 (105 top 1 ranks with ChemSpider). This is also indicated by the higher relative ranking positions (RRP) [<a href="#CR19" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR19">19</a>] retrieved by MetFrag2.2 where a value of 1 marks the best possible result and 0 the worst possible result. Note that the version used here is 1-RRP as defined in Kerber et al. [<a href="#CR54" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR54">54</a>] and Schymanski et al. [<a href="#CR55" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR55">55</a>]. The results show that the algorithmic refinements improved the baseline <em>in silico </em>fragmentation performance, although it is difficult to tell which of the changes had the greatest influence.</p>

<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/Tab1/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<div class="tw-foot p"><div class="fn" id="_fn_p34"><p>MetFrag2010 and MetFrag2.2 were compared with the same ChemSpider candidate sets; MetFrag2.2 and CFM-ID with the same PubChem candidate sets. Far right: Best top 1 pessimistic ranks obtained by combining MetFrag2.2 and CFM-ID 2.0 with the weights <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq26"><math id="M142" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi mathvariant="italic">ω</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">Frag</mi></msub><mo>=</mo><mn>0.67</mn></mrow></math></span> and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq27"><math id="M144" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi mathvariant="italic">ω</mi><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">CFM</mi><mtext>-</mtext><mi mathvariant="normal">ID</mi></mrow></msub><mo>=</mo><mn>0.33</mn></mrow></math></span>. The expected ranks, which partially account for equally scored candidates as calculated in [<a href="#CR16" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR16">16</a>], are shown in the lower part of the table</p></div></div></section></section><section id="Sec15"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">Comparison with CFM-ID using Orbitrap XL Dataset</h4>
<p>The same dataset of 473 merged spectra and the corresponding PubChem candidate sets were used as input for CFM-ID [<a href="#CR16" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR16">16</a>] version 2.0 (“Jaccard”, RDKit 2015.03.1, lpsolve 5.5.2.0, Boost 1.55.0), to form a baseline comparison with an alternative <em>in silico </em>fragmenter. The results, given in Table <a href="#Tab1" class="usa-link">1</a>, show that CFM-ID generally performed better, indicated by the higher number of correct first ranked candidates (43 vs. 30), top 5 (170 vs. 145), top 10 (232 vs. 226) and a lower median and mean rank of 11 versus 12 and 127 versus 141. The expected ranks, including equal ranked candidates, also implied a better performance of CFM-ID (top 1: 43 vs. 57, top 5: 163 vs. 193, top 10: 245 vs. 261). This was not entirely unexpected as CFM-ID uses a more sophisticated fragmentation approach, but also requires a much longer computation time. For run time analysis, 84 of the 473 queries, selected at random, were processed (single-threaded) with MetFrag2.2 and CFM-ID in parallel on a computer cluster with a maximum of 28 (virtual) computer nodes with 12 CPU cores each. The total run times (system + user runtime, retrieved by linux bash command <em>time</em>) were 75 min for MetFrag2.2 and 12,570 min (209.5 h) for CFM-ID. These values represent the runtime on a single CPU core for all 84 queries in series. The average run time per query amounts to 54 s for MetFrag2.2 and 8979 s (150 min) for CFM-ID.</p>
<p>As CFM-ID and MetFrag2.2 use independent <em>in silico </em>fragmentation approaches, one can hypothesize that the combination of the approaches should improve the results further. To demonstrate this, the CFM-ID results were incorporated into MetFrag2.2 by introducing an additional scoring term <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq111"><math id="M146" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi mathvariant="italic">ω</mi><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">CFM</mi><mtext>-</mtext><mi mathvariant="normal">ID</mi></mrow></msub><mo>·</mo><msub><mi>S</mi><msub><mi>C</mi><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">CFM</mi><mtext>-</mtext><mi mathvariant="normal">ID</mi></mrow></msub></msub></mrow></math></span>, where <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq112"><math id="M148" overflow="linebreak"><msub><mi>S</mi><msub><mi>C</mi><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">CFM</mi><mtext>-</mtext><mi mathvariant="normal">ID</mi></mrow></msub></msub></math></span> defines the normalized CFM-ID probability of candidate <em>C</em>. Different contributions of each fragmenter relative to another was determined by randomly drawing 100 combinations of <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq113"><math id="M150" overflow="linebreak"><msub><mi mathvariant="italic">ω</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">Frag</mi></msub></math></span> and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq114"><math id="M152" overflow="linebreak"><msub><mi mathvariant="italic">ω</mi><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">CFM</mi><mtext>-</mtext><mi mathvariant="normal">ID</mi></mrow></msub></math></span> such that (<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq115"><math id="M154" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi mathvariant="italic">ω</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">Frag</mi></msub><mo>+</mo><msub><mi mathvariant="italic">ω</mi><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">CFM</mi><mtext>-</mtext><mi mathvariant="normal">ID</mi></mrow></msub><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></math></span>). The best results, shown in Table <a href="#Tab1" class="usa-link">1</a>, were obtained with <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq116"><math id="M156" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi mathvariant="italic">ω</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">Frag</mi></msub><mo>=</mo><mn>0.67</mn></mrow></math></span> and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq117"><math id="M158" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi mathvariant="italic">ω</mi><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">CFM</mi><mtext>-</mtext><mi mathvariant="normal">ID</mi></mrow></msub><mo>=</mo><mn>0.33</mn></mrow></math></span>, where the change in number 1 ranks with weight is shown in Additional file <a href="#MOESM4" class="usa-link">4</a>. With this best combination, the number of Top 1 ranks improved from 30 to 61, while the median rank improved to 8. This shows that the combination of independent fragmentation methods can indeed yield valuable improvements to the results, shown again in the next paragraph after including the additional information. Further validation was beyond the scope of the current article, as further improvements could be made by retraining CFM-ID on Orbitrap data, but would be of interest in the future.</p></section></section><section id="Sec16"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Adding retention time and reference information</h3>
<section id="Sec17"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">Parameter selection on Orbitrap XL Dataset</h4>
<p>The next stage was to assess the effect of references and retention time information on the MetFrag results. Firstly, each score term (i.e. fragmenter, retention time and/or reference information) was either included or excluded by setting the weight (<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq118"><math id="M160" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi mathvariant="italic">ω</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">Frag</mi></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi mathvariant="italic">ω</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">RT</mi></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi mathvariant="italic">ω</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">Refs</mi></msub></mrow></math></span>) to 1 or 0, to assess the impact of the various combinations on the number of correctly-ranked number 1 substances. The results are shown in Table <a href="#Tab2" class="usa-link">2</a>. The best result was obtained when all three “score terms” (fragmenter, RT and references) were included in candidate ranking. For PubChem, both RT/log <em>P</em> models (CDK XlogP and XLOGP3 from PubChem directly) were assessed and thus two sets of results are reported. The reference information was included using the combined reference scores introduced in Eqs. <a href="#Equ6" class="usa-link">6</a> and <a href="#Equ8" class="usa-link">8</a>, where all combinations of the reference values described above (1–2 for PubChem, 1–5 for ChemSpider, i.e. 3 and 31 combinations in total, respectively), were used to form a cumulative total reference term, shown in Eq. <a href="#Equ5" class="usa-link">5</a> for PubChem and Eq. <a href="#Equ7" class="usa-link">7</a> for ChemSpider. The best results were achieved with PubChem when using both patents and PubMed references (<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq119"><math id="M162" overflow="linebreak"><msub><mi>S</mi><msub><mi>C</mi><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">PNP</mi><mo>+</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">PPC</mi></mrow></msub></msub></math></span>; <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq120"><math id="M164" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>a</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></math></span>, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq121"><math id="M166" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>a</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></math></span>), while for ChemSpider using the ReferenceCount, ExternalReferenceCount and the DataSourceCount (<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq122"><math id="M168" overflow="linebreak"><msub><mi>S</mi><msub><mi>C</mi><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">CRC</mi><mo>+</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">CERC</mi><mo>+</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">CDC</mi></mrow></msub></msub></math></span>) proved best, i.e. <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq123"><math id="M170" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>b</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>b</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>b</mi><mn>3</mn></msub><mo>=</mo><mn>0</mn><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>b</mi><mn>4</mn></msub><mo>=</mo><mn>0</mn><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>b</mi><mn>5</mn></msub><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></math></span>. Table <a href="#Tab2" class="usa-link">2</a> contains the number of Top 1 ranks for each combination of <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq124"><math id="M172" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi mathvariant="italic">ω</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">Frag</mi></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi mathvariant="italic">ω</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">RT</mi></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi mathvariant="italic">ω</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">Refs</mi></msub><mo>∈</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">{</mo><mn>0</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>1</mn><mo stretchy="false">}</mo></mrow></mrow></math></span>. The results show clearly that, while references alone result in over 311 top 1 ranks (65 % for PubChem), the addition of both fragmentation and retention time information improves the results further, to 69 % of candidates ranked first (PubChem) and even 87 % of candidates ranked first (ChemSpider). For PubChem the distribution of the number of CombinedReferences (including patents and PubMed references) for the 359 queries of the (unique) correct candidates is shown in Additional file <a href="#MOESM5" class="usa-link">5</a>.</p>

<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/Tab2/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<div class="tw-foot p"><div class="fn" id="_fn_p39"><p>The weights indicate where the score term was included (1) or excluded (0) from the candidate ranking. For PubChem <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq34"><math id="M186" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi mathvariant="italic">ω</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">Refs</mi></msub><mo>·</mo><msub><mi>S</mi><msub><mi>C</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">Refs</mi></msub></msub><mo>=</mo><msub><mi mathvariant="italic">ω</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">Refs</mi></msub><mo>·</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>S</mi><msub><mi>C</mi><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">PNP</mi><mo>+</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">PPC</mi></mrow></msub></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow></math></span>; for ChemSpider <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq35"><math id="M188" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>S</mi><msub><mi>C</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">Refs</mi></msub></msub><mo>=</mo><msub><mi>S</mi><msub><mi>C</mi><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">CRC</mi><mo>+</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">CERC</mi><mo>+</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">CDC</mi></mrow></msub></msub></mrow></math></span> only. See text for explanations</p></div></div></section><p>Following this, the combination of each scoring term was assessed by randomly drawing 1000 different weight combinations such that (<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq125"><math id="M190" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi mathvariant="italic">ω</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">Frag</mi></msub><mo>+</mo><msub><mi mathvariant="italic">ω</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">RT</mi></msub><mo>+</mo><msub><mi mathvariant="italic">ω</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">Refs</mi></msub><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></math></span>) to determine the optimal relative contributions of each term for the best results. This was performed for all combinations of reference sources (3 for PubChem, 31 for ChemSpider). The best result was obtained again when using both patents and PubMed references for PubChem (<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq126"><math id="M192" overflow="linebreak"><msub><mi>S</mi><msub><mi>C</mi><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">PNP</mi><mo>+</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">PPC</mi></mrow></msub></msub></math></span>; <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq127"><math id="M194" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>a</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></math></span>, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq128"><math id="M196" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>a</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></math></span>), but using only the ReferenceCount (<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq129"><math id="M198" overflow="linebreak"><msub><mi>S</mi><msub><mi>C</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">CRC</mi></msub></msub></math></span>; <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq130"><math id="M200" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>b</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></math></span>, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq131"><math id="M202" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>b</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><mo>=</mo><mn>0</mn></mrow></math></span>, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq132"><math id="M204" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>b</mi><mn>3</mn></msub><mo>=</mo><mn>0</mn></mrow></math></span>, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq133"><math id="M206" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>b</mi><mn>4</mn></msub><mo>=</mo><mn>0</mn></mrow></math></span>, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq134"><math id="M208" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>b</mi><mn>5</mn></msub><mo>=</mo><mn>0</mn></mrow></math></span>) for ChemSpider. The results are summarized in Table <a href="#Tab3" class="usa-link">3</a> (including the weight terms) and shown in Figs. <a href="#Fig1" class="usa-link">1</a> and <a href="#Fig2" class="usa-link">2</a> for PubChem and ChemSpider respectively. These triangle plots show the top 1 candidates for all <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq135"><math id="M210" overflow="linebreak"><msub><mi mathvariant="italic">ω</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></math></span> combinations, colour-coded (black—0 % of the correct candidates ranked first, yellow—10 0 % of the correct candidates ranked first) with the <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq136"><math id="M212" overflow="linebreak"><msub><mi mathvariant="italic">ω</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></math></span> per category increasing in the direction of the arrow. Each corner is <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq137"><math id="M214" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi mathvariant="italic">ω</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></math></span>. The 25th and 75th percentiles are shown to give an idea of the distribution of the ranks. The equivalent plots for the number of top 5 and top 10 ranks are given in Additional files <a href="#MOESM6" class="usa-link">6</a>, <a href="#MOESM7" class="usa-link">7</a>, <a href="#MOESM8" class="usa-link">8</a> and <a href="#MOESM9" class="usa-link">9</a>. Although the results from (<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq138"><math id="M216" overflow="linebreak"><msub><mi mathvariant="italic">ω</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">Frag</mi></msub></math></span>, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq139"><math id="M218" overflow="linebreak"><msub><mi mathvariant="italic">ω</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">RT</mi></msub></math></span>, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq140"><math id="M220" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi mathvariant="italic">ω</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">Refs</mi></msub><mo>∈</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">{</mo><mn>0</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>1</mn><mo stretchy="false">}</mo></mrow></mrow></math></span>) above indicated that the term <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq141"><math id="M222" overflow="linebreak"><msub><mi>S</mi><msub><mi>C</mi><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">CRC</mi><mo>+</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">CERC</mi><mo>+</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">CDC</mi></mrow></msub></msub></math></span> yielded the best result for ChemSpider with 411 top 1 ranks, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq142"><math id="M224" overflow="linebreak"><msub><mi>S</mi><msub><mi>C</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">CRC</mi></msub></msub></math></span> yielded 410 top 1 ranks for the same calculations, indicating that there is little difference between the two combinations. Using the randomly-drawn weights, the top 1 ranks improved to 420 (ChemSpider) and 336 (PubChem). This proves without a doubt that the addition of reference and retention time information drastically improves the performance, going from 22 to 89 % top 1 ranks (ChemSpider) and 6.3 to 71 % (PubChem).</p>
