Far right: combining CFM-ID results to incorporate complementary fragmentation information</p></div></div></section><figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="Fig1"><h5 class="obj_head">Fig. 1.</h5>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/d2eb/4732001/edea381f8c80/13321_2016_115_Fig1_HTML.jpg" loading="lazy" id="MO1" height="518" width="615" alt="Fig. 1"></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/Fig1/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Top 1 ranks with PubChem (XlogP3) on the Orbitrap XL Dataset. The results were obtained with MetFrag formula query and the inclusion of references and retention time. The reference score was calculated with the number of patents (PNP) and PubMed references (PPC). The <em>larger dots</em> show the best result (336 number 1 ranks), 75th percentile (320), median (312), 25th percentile (249) and worst result (61). For the best result, the weights were <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq17"><math id="M248" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi mathvariant="italic">ω</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">Frag</mi></msub><mo>=</mo><mn>0.50</mn><mo>,</mo><msub><mi mathvariant="italic">ω</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">RT</mi></msub><mo>=</mo><mn>0.16</mn></mrow></math></span> and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq18"><math id="M250" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi mathvariant="italic">ω</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">Refs</mi></msub><mo>=</mo><mn>0.34</mn></mrow></math></span>
</p></figcaption></figure><figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="Fig2"><h5 class="obj_head">Fig. 2.</h5>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/d2eb/4732001/63a51514d83e/13321_2016_115_Fig2_HTML.jpg" loading="lazy" id="MO2" height="538" width="638" alt="Fig. 2"></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/Fig2/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Top 1 ranks with ChemSpider on the Orbitrap XL Dataset. The results were obtained with MetFrag formula query and the inclusion of references and retention time. The reference score was calculated with the ChemSpider reference count (CRC). The <em>larger dots</em> show the best result (420), 75th percentile (399), median (388), 25th percentile (311) and worst result (104). The weights for the best result were <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq19"><math id="M252" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi mathvariant="italic">ω</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">Frag</mi></msub><mo>=</mo><mn>0.49</mn><mo>,</mo><msub><mi mathvariant="italic">ω</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">RT</mi></msub><mo>=</mo><mn>0.19</mn></mrow></math></span> and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq20"><math id="M254" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi mathvariant="italic">ω</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">Refs</mi></msub><mo>=</mo><mn>0.32</mn></mrow></math></span>
</p></figcaption></figure><p>As above, it was interesting to investigate whether the addition of a complementary fragmentation technique, i.e. CFM-ID, would improve the results even further. MetFrag2.2 and CFM-ID were combined with retention time and reference information using 1000 randomly drawn combinations of <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq143"><math id="M256" overflow="linebreak"><msub><mi mathvariant="italic">ω</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">Frag</mi></msub></math></span>, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq144"><math id="M258" overflow="linebreak"><msub><mi mathvariant="italic">ω</mi><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">CFM</mi><mtext>-</mtext><mi mathvariant="normal">ID</mi></mrow></msub></math></span>, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq145"><math id="M260" overflow="linebreak"><msub><mi mathvariant="italic">ω</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">RT</mi></msub></math></span> and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq146"><math id="M262" overflow="linebreak"><msub><mi mathvariant="italic">ω</mi><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">PNP</mi><mo>+</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">PPC</mi></mrow></msub></math></span> such that (<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq147"><math id="M264" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi mathvariant="italic">ω</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">Frag</mi></msub><mo>+</mo><msub><mi mathvariant="italic">ω</mi><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">CFM</mi><mtext>-</mtext><mi mathvariant="normal">ID</mi></mrow></msub><mo>+</mo><msub><mi mathvariant="italic">ω</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">RT</mi></msub></mrow></math></span> + <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq148"><math id="M266" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi mathvariant="italic">ω</mi><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">PNP</mi><mo>+</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">PPC</mi></mrow></msub><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></math></span>). The results, shown in Table <a href="#Tab3" class="usa-link">3</a>, indicate that the PubChem results can be improved further, to 343 top 1 ranks (73 %). This is a drastic improvement from the performance of both original fragmenters alone, with CFM-ID alone yielding between 10 and 12 % top 1 hits (expected rank) in their original publication [<a href="#CR16" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR16">16</a>] with an older PubChem, the combination of both fragmenters alone yielding 15 % (expected rank) here. These combined results are also drastically better than the latest <em>in silico </em>fragmentation results just published for CSI:FingerID. Dührkop et al. [<a href="#CR18" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR18">18</a>] investigated each individual fragmenter currently available and compared the results with the CSI:FingerID. Despite using different data and settings to those here, their results on the Agilent dataset indicated that MetFrag2010 and CFM-ID achieved 9 and 12 % top 1 (expected) ranks, which are reasonably comparable with the results presented above. FingerID [<a href="#CR15" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR15">15</a>] achieved 19.6 %, while CSI:FingerID achieved 39 % top 1 results, which is a dramatic improvement over the other fragmenters. Since the external information boosted the top 1 ranks to 73 % for MetFrag2.2 plus CFM-ID, one could speculate that the combination of CSI:FingerID, MetFrag2.2 and CFM-ID would result in an even greater performance.</p></section><section id="Sec18"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">Cross-evaluation on additional datasets</h4>
<p>As the RT and reference scores are very subjective to experimental context, MetFrag2.2 now contains so many tuneable parameters that it will be beneficial to users when a few default cases are suggested. Thus, once the optimal reference source combinations were determined as described above, alternative datasets were used to re-determine the optimal weights <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq149"><math id="M268" overflow="linebreak"><msub><mi mathvariant="italic">ω</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">Frag</mi></msub></math></span>, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq150"><math id="M270" overflow="linebreak"><msub><mi mathvariant="italic">ω</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">RT</mi></msub></math></span> and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq151"><math id="M272" overflow="linebreak"><msub><mi mathvariant="italic">ω</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">Refs</mi></msub></math></span> to investigate the variation over different datasets. Three sufficiently large datasets available on MassBank contained good quality MS/MS and RT data, all processed with RMassBank [<a href="#CR51" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR51">51</a>].</p>
<p><em>UF dataset:</em> A susbset of the 2758 UFZ Orbitrap XL records were acquired on an Kinetex Core-Shell C18 column from Phenomenex with a 40 min chromatographic program (all others were direct infusion experiments). These MS/MS spectra, arising from [M + H]<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq153"><math id="M274" overflow="linebreak"><msup><mrow></mrow><mo>+</mo></msup></math></span> and [M − H]<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq155"><math id="M276" overflow="linebreak"><msup><mrow></mrow><mo>-</mo></msup></math></span> precursors, were recorded at four collision energies: CID 35 and 55 as well as HCD 50 and 80. These spectra were merged and processed as described above for the Orbitrap XL dataset, resulting in 225 merged spectra (“UF” dataset) from 195 substances (184 [M + H]<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq157"><math id="M278" overflow="linebreak"><msup><mrow></mrow><mo>+</mo></msup></math></span> and 41 [M <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq158"><math id="M280" overflow="linebreak"><mo>-</mo></math></span> H]<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq159"><math id="M282" overflow="linebreak"><msup><mrow></mrow><mo>-</mo></msup></math></span>).</p>
<p><em>EQex and EQxPlus datasets:</em> Two additional Eawag datasets were also available. The “EQex” dataset, measured on a Q Exactive Orbitrap, contained MS/MS spectra associated with [M + H]<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq161"><math id="M284" overflow="linebreak"><msup><mrow></mrow><mo>+</mo></msup></math></span> and [M − H]<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq163"><math id="M286" overflow="linebreak"><msup><mrow></mrow><mo>-</mo></msup></math></span> precursors recorded at six different collision energies (HCD 15, 30, 45, 60, 75 and 90). The “EQExPlus” dataset, measured on a Q Exactive Plus Orbitrap, contained MS/MS spectra associated with [M + H]<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq165"><math id="M288" overflow="linebreak"><msup><mrow></mrow><mo>+</mo></msup></math></span> and [M − H]<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq167"><math id="M290" overflow="linebreak"><msup><mrow></mrow><mo>-</mo></msup></math></span> precursors recorded at nine different collision energies (HCD 15, 30, 45, 60, 75, 90, 120, 150, 180).</p>
<p>Both datasets were acquired using the same LC set-up as the other Eawag dataset. The MS/MS from these two datasets were merged as above to yield 294 merged spectra from 204 compounds (195 [M <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq168"><math id="M292" overflow="linebreak"><mo>+</mo></math></span> H]<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq169"><math id="M294" overflow="linebreak"><msup><mrow></mrow><mo>+</mo></msup></math></span> and 94 [M − H]<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq171"><math id="M296" overflow="linebreak"><msup><mrow></mrow><mo>-</mo></msup></math></span>) for the “EQEx” dataset and 314 merged spectra from 232 compounds (219 [M + H]<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq173"><math id="M298" overflow="linebreak"><msup><mrow></mrow><mo>+</mo></msup></math></span> and 91 [M − H]<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq175"><math id="M300" overflow="linebreak"><msup><mrow></mrow><mo>-</mo></msup></math></span>) for the “EQExPlus” dataset. There was a very small overlap between the different Eawag datasets (5, 2 and 2 substance overlap between EA and EQEx, EA and EQExPlus and EQEx and EQExPlus, respectively).</p>
<p>The overlap between the UFZ and Eawag datasets was larger, with 97, 16 and 21 substances in common between the UFZ and EA, EQEx and EQExPlus datasets, respectively. The overlap was determined using the first block of the InChIKey. As the spectral and retention time data for the substances in the individual datasets were processed independently with different collision energies and ionization modes, none of the overlapping substances were removed from the datasets. The retention times extracted from the MassBank records per substance were used to establish the RT–log <em>P</em> model (see Eq. <a href="#Equ9" class="usa-link">9</a>) for each dataset independently based on a tenfold cross-validation.</p>
<p>The influence of the different parameters was assessed for each dataset by setting <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq176"><math id="M302" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi mathvariant="italic">ω</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">Frag</mi></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi mathvariant="italic">ω</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">RT</mi></msub></mrow></math></span> and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq177"><math id="M304" overflow="linebreak"><msub><mi mathvariant="italic">ω</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">Refs</mi></msub></math></span> to either 0 or 1 again; these results are presented in Table <a href="#Tab4" class="usa-link">4</a>. As above, the performance improved from between 2 and 9 % of the candidates ranked first using fragmentation alone, through to 64–82 % ranked first when all <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq178"><math id="M306" overflow="linebreak"><msub><mi mathvariant="italic">ω</mi><mi>x</mi></msub></math></span> were weighted equally, although the results varied quite dramatically between the datasets. The 473 spectrum dataset used above thus fell within this range.</p>

<div class="tw-foot p"><div class="fn" id="_fn_p53"><p>The weights indicate where ranking parameters were included (1) or excluded (0) from the candidate ranking. Retention time score calculation was performed using the XLOGP3 values of PubChem. <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq74"><math id="M320" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi mathvariant="italic">ω</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">Refs</mi></msub><mo>·</mo><msub><mi>S</mi><msub><mi>C</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">Refs</mi></msub></msub><mo>=</mo><msub><mi mathvariant="italic">ω</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">Refs</mi></msub><mo>·</mo><msub><mi>S</mi><msub><mi>C</mi><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">PNP</mi><mo>+</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">PPC</mi></mrow></msub></msub></mrow></math></span>. See text for explanations</p></div></div></section><p>Similarly, the optimization of <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq179"><math id="M322" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi mathvariant="italic">ω</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">Frag</mi></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi mathvariant="italic">ω</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">RT</mi></msub></mrow></math></span> and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq180"><math id="M324" overflow="linebreak"><msub><mi mathvariant="italic">ω</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">Refs</mi></msub></math></span> was performed again for each dataset independently using the 1000 randomly-drawn weights. The results are presented in Table <a href="#Tab5" class="usa-link">5</a> and show that the percentage of top 1 ranks varies widely between the datasets, from 63 to 82 %; the original dataset falls in the middle with 71 %. The results in Table <a href="#Tab5" class="usa-link">5</a> also show that the suggested relative weights to one another remain consistent enough to enable default parameter suggestion, with <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq181"><math id="M326" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi mathvariant="italic">ω</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">Frag</mi></msub><mo>≈</mo><mn>0.5</mn><mo>,</mo><msub><mi mathvariant="italic">ω</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">RT</mi></msub><mo>≈</mo><mn>0.2</mn></mrow></math></span> and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq182"><math id="M328" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi mathvariant="italic">ω</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">Refs</mi></msub><mo>≈</mo><mn>0.3</mn></mrow></math></span>. All results for the number of top 1 ranks for the three additional datasets are shown in Additional files <a href="#MOESM10" class="usa-link">10</a>, <a href="#MOESM11" class="usa-link">11</a> and <a href="#MOESM12" class="usa-link">12</a>.</p>

<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/Tab5/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<div class="tw-foot p"><div class="fn" id="_fn_p56"><p>Retention time score calculation was performed using the XLOGP3 values of PubChem. <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq92"><math id="M344" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi mathvariant="italic">ω</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">Refs</mi></msub><mo>·</mo><msub><mi>S</mi><msub><mi>C</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">Refs</mi></msub></msub><mo>=</mo><msub><mi mathvariant="italic">ω</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">Refs</mi></msub><mo>·</mo><msub><mi>S</mi><msub><mi>C</mi><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">PNP</mi><mo>+</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">PPC</mi></mrow></msub></msub></mrow></math></span>. See text for explanations</p></div></div></section></section></section><section id="Sec19"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Specific examples</h3>
<p>As the additional features are more difficult to evaluate using large datasets, individual examples are presented below to demonstrate the flexibility of MetFrag2.2 command line (CL), with the corresponding commands give in a different font. Lists of the available parameters are given in Additional files <a href="#MOESM1" class="usa-link">1</a>, <a href="#MOESM2" class="usa-link">2</a> and <a href="#MOESM3" class="usa-link">3</a>. These examples serve to show how MetFrag2.2 can also be adjusted by the user to explore individual cases in greater detail than during e.g. a high-throughput screening.</p>
<section id="Sec20"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">Gathering evidence for unknown 199.0428</h4>
<p>During the NORMAN Collaborative Non-target Screening Trial [<a href="#CR1" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR1">1</a>], a tentatively identified non-target substance of <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> [M − H]<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq184"><math id="M346" overflow="linebreak"><msup><mrow></mrow><mo>-</mo></msup></math></span> 199.0431 was reported by one participant as mesitylenesulfonic acid (ChemSpider ID (CSID) 69438, formula <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq185"><math id="M348" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mtext>C</mtext><mn>9</mn></msub><msub><mtext>H</mtext><mn>12</mn></msub><msub><mtext>O</mtext><mn>3</mn></msub><mtext>S</mtext></mrow></math></span>, neutral monoisotopic mass 200.0507) or isomer. The same unknown was detected in the same sample measured at a second institute, where the standard of mesitylenesulfonic acid was available. Although the retention time was plausible (5.96 min), comparing the MS/MS spectra clearly disproved the proposed identification, with many fragments from the unknown absent in the standard spectrum. Thus, MetFrag2.2 was used to investigate other possibilities.</p>
<p>Firstly, the following parameter combination was used, taking the unknown MS/MS peak list from the second participant: ChemSpider exact mass search, fragment error 0.001 Da + 5 ppm, tree depth 2, unconnected compound and InChIKey filter, filter included elements = C, S (as isotope signals were detected in the full scan), experimental RT = 6.20 min, an RT training set of 355 InChIs and RTs measured on the same system and score weights of 1 (fragmenter and RT score) and 0.25 each for four ChemSpider reference sources. This yielded 134 candidates with four different formulas (<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq186"><math id="M350" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mtext>C</mtext><mn>9</mn></msub><msub><mtext>H</mtext><mn>12</mn></msub><msub><mtext>O</mtext><mn>3</mn></msub><mtext>S</mtext><mo>,</mo><mrow><msub><mtext>C</mtext><mn>8</mn></msub><msub><mtext>H</mtext><mn>16</mn></msub><mtext>S</mtext><msub><mtext>Si</mtext><mn>2</mn></msub></mrow><mo>,</mo><mrow><msub><mtext>C</mtext><mn>7</mn></msub><msub><mtext>H</mtext><mn>13</mn></msub><msub><mtext>BO</mtext><mn>2</mn></msub><mtext>SSi</mtext></mrow><mo>,</mo><mrow><msub><mtext>C</mtext><mn>7</mn></msub><msub><mtext>H</mtext><mn>10</mn></msub><msub><mtext>N</mtext><mn>3</mn></msub><msub><mtext>O</mtext><mn>2</mn></msub><mtext>S</mtext></mrow></mrow></math></span>), all fulfiling the element filter (C, S). <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq187"><math id="M352" overflow="linebreak"><msub><mi>S</mi><msub><mi>C</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">Final</mi></msub></msub></math></span> ranged from 0.70 to 2.12, where several candidates had high numbers of references and similar number of peaks explained. Three candidates are shown in Table <a href="#Tab6" class="usa-link">6</a>, along with a summary of the information retrieved. The clear top match, ethyl <em>p</em>-toluenesulfonate (CSID 6386, shown to the left) was unlikely to be correct, as the MS/MS contained no evidence of an ethyl loss and also had a clear fragment peak at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 79.9556, corresponding with an <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq188"><math id="M354" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mtext>SO</mtext><mn>3</mn></msub><mtext>H</mtext></mrow></math></span> group (thus eliminating alkyl sulfonates from consideration).</p>

<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/Tab6/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<div class="tw-foot p"><div class="fn" id="_fn_p61"><p>Structures overlaid with the included substructure were generated with AMBIT [<a href="#CR57" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR57">57</a>]. See text for details</p></div></div></section><p>MetFrag2.2 was run again with the SMARTS substructure inclusion filter, which resulted in 31 candidates but with the same top matching structure. However, adding the SMARTS S(=O)(=O)OC to the exclusion list eliminates the alkyl sulfonate species and resulted in 18 candidates, where the top candidate was now the originally proposed (and rejected) identification mesitylenesulfonic acid, shown in the middle of Table <a href="#Tab6" class="usa-link">6</a>. The next matches were substitution isomers. Referring to the MS/MS again, another large peak was present at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 183.0115, which is often observed in surfactant spectra corresponding with a <em>p</em>-ethyl benzenesulfonic acid moiety. Running MetFrag2.2 again with a substructure inclusion of CCc1ccc(cc1)S(=O)(=O)O yielded only two candidates, 4-isopropylbenzenesulfonic acid (<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq189"><math id="M366" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>S</mi><msub><mi>C</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">Final</mi></msub></msub><mo>=</mo><mn>2.5</mn><mo>,</mo></mrow></math></span> CSID 6388), shown to the right in Table <a href="#Tab6" class="usa-link">6</a> and 4-propylbenzenesulfonic acid (<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq190"><math id="M368" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>S</mi><msub><mi>C</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">Final</mi></msub></msub><mo>=</mo><mn>2.0</mn><mo>,</mo></mrow></math></span> CSID 5506213).</p>
<p>To check the relevance of the proposed candidates in an environmental sample, a “suspect screening” was performed. The STOFF-IDENT database [<a href="#CR24" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR24">24</a>] contains over 8000 substances including those in high volume production and use in Europe registered under the European REACH (Registration, Evaluation, Authorisation and Restriction of CHemicals) Legislation. The STOFF-IDENT contents were downloaded (February 2015) and the SMILES were converted to InChIKeys using OpenBabel and given as input to MetFrag as a suspect list. Of the 134 original candidates, only one, 4-isopropylbenzenesulfonic acid, was tagged as being present in the STOFF-IDENT database. This gives additional evidence that indeed 4-isopropylbenzenesulfonic acid is the substance behind the unknown spectrum, however it has not been possible to confirm this identification at this stage due to the lack of a sufficiently pure reference standard.</p></section><section id="Sec21"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">Terbutylazine and isobars</h4>
<p>The example of terbutylazine (CSID 20848, see Table <a href="#Tab7" class="usa-link">7</a>) shows how MetFrag2.2 can help in gathering the evidence supporting the identification of isobaric substances. Terbutylazine and secbutylazine (CSID 22172) often co-elute in generic non-target chromatographic methods and have very similar fragmentation patterns, but can usually be distinguished from the other common triazine isobars propazine (CSID 4768) and triethazine (CSID 15157) via MS/MS information. However, during the NORMAN non-target screening collaborative trial [<a href="#CR1" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR1">1</a>], all four substances were reported as potential matches for the same mass, showing clearly the danger of suspect screening based only on exact mass. For this example, the merged [M + H]<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq209"><math id="M370" overflow="linebreak"><msup><mrow></mrow><mo>+</mo></msup></math></span>MS/MS spectrum of terbutylazine from the EA dataset above (EA02840X) was used as a peak list to run MetFrag2.2, as the correct answer is clear with a reference spectrum. Table <a href="#Tab7" class="usa-link">7</a> shows the data for the four substances mentioned above plus the top match based on fragmentation data alone, <em>N</em>-butyl-6-chloro-<em>N</em><span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq210"><math id="M372" overflow="linebreak"><msup><mrow></mrow><mo>′</mo></msup></math></span>-ethyl-1,3,5-triazine-2,4-diamine (CSID 4954587, given the synonym “<em>n</em>Butylazine” hereafter to save space). ChemSpider was used to perform an exact mass search, resulting in a total of 112 structures (data from only five are shown). Five scores were used, all with weight 1: FragmenterScore, ChemSpiderReferenceCount, RetentionTimeScore, SuspectListsScore and SmartsSubstructureInclusionScore. To show the inclusion of external log <em>P</em> calculations, ChemAxon JChem for Excel [<a href="#CR56" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR56">56</a>] was used to predict log <em>P</em> and log <em>D</em> at pH 6.8 (the pH of the chromatographic program used) for a training dataset of the 810 substances in the Eawag database on MassBank. The log <em>P</em> and log <em>D</em> predictions were then performed externally for all MetFrag candidates on the dominant tautomeric species and added to the MetFrag CSV file for final scoring. The scores, shown in Table <a href="#Tab7" class="usa-link">7</a>, showed that different candidates were the best match for different categories, indicated in italics. The candidates are ordered by the number of references. As above, STOFF-IDENT was used as a suspect list and all four of the substances reported by trial participants were indeed in STOFF-IDENT. However, Table <a href="#Tab7" class="usa-link">7</a> clearly shows that two can be eliminated using <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq211"><math id="M374" overflow="linebreak"><msub><mi>S</mi><msub><mi>C</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">Frag</mi></msub></msub></math></span> and substructure matches (as the MS/MS clearly displays the loss of a <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq212"><math id="M376" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mtext>C</mtext><mn>2</mn></msub><msub><mtext>H</mtext><mn>5</mn></msub></mrow></math></span> and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq213"><math id="M378" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mtext>C</mtext><mn>4</mn></msub><msub><mtext>H</mtext><mn>9</mn></msub></mrow></math></span> group, indicating these are likey attached to a heteroatom, in this case N). Although secbutylazine is scored lower than terbutylazine, the reference count is the main influence here and both substances could be present in an environmental sample—depending on the context.</p>

<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/Tab7/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<div class="tw-foot p"><div class="fn" id="_fn_p66">
<p>The predicted log <em>P</em> and log <em>D</em> from the retention time was 3.17 and 2.18 using a training set of 810 substances calculated externally with ChemAxon and added to MetFrag2.2 via the UserLogP option. Included substructure SMARTS were N[CH<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq205"><math id="M408" overflow="linebreak"><msub><mrow></mrow><mn>2</mn></msub></math></span>][CH<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq206"><math id="M410" overflow="linebreak"><msub><mrow></mrow><mn>3</mn></msub></math></span>], NCCCC, NC(C)CC, NC(C)(C)C</p>
<p>
<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq207"><math id="M412" overflow="linebreak"><msup><mrow></mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">a</mi></msup></math></span>Name synonym assigned for space reasons. The values in italics indicates the best result per category. Structures overlaid with the included substructure were generated with AMBIT [<a href="#CR57" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR57">57</a>]. See text for details and weights</p>
</div></div></section><p>The large dataset evaluations show that MetFrag2.2 is suitable for high-throughput workflows, with a relatively quick runtime. On the other hand, the detailed examples shows how the various features of MetFrag2.2 can be used to investigate the top candidates in more detail and enhance the interpretation of the results, including the inclusion of external RT/log <em>P</em> and/or log <em>D</em> information that cannot be calculated within MetFrag2.2 (e.g. due to license restrictions, as in the case of ChemAxon).</p></section></section></section><section id="Sec22"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Conclusions</h2>
<p>In many cases additional information is available and needed from the experimental context to complement small molecule identification, especially where the mass spectrum alone is not sufficient for candidate selection from a large number of candidates. The results for MetFrag2.2 clearly show the benefit of considering this additional information, with a tenfold improvement compared with MetFrag2.2 fragmentation information alone. The flexibility of the new features in addition to the ability to add user-defined scores means that MetFrag2.2 is ideally suited to high-throughput workflows, but can also be used to perform individual elucidation efforts in greater detail. The ability to incorporate CFM-ID as an additional scoring function shows the potential to improve these results further using complementary <em>in silico </em>fragmentation approaches. The parameter files including the spectral data, the candidate, result and ranking files of the used EA, UF, EQEx, EQExPlus and HILL datasets are available at <a href="http://msbi.ipb-halle.de/~cruttkie/CHIN-D-15-00088/" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">http://msbi.ipb-halle.de/download/CHIN-D-15-00088/</a> and can be downloaded as ZIP archives. Feedback on the command line version available at <a href="http://c-ruttkies.github.io/MetFrag/" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">http://c-ruttkies.github.io/MetFrag/</a> is welcome. The new functions greatly reduce the burden on users to collect and merge ever increasing amounts of information available for substances present in different compound databases, thus enabling them to consider much more evidence during their screening efforts.</p></section><section id="Sec23"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Availability and requirements</h2>
<ul class="list" style="list-style-type:disc">
<li><p>Project name: MetFrag2.2;</p></li>
<li><p>Project home page: <a href="http://c-ruttkies.github.io/MetFrag/" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">http://c-ruttkies.github.io/MetFrag/</a>;</p></li>
<li><p>Operating system(s): Platform independent;</p></li>
<li><p>Programming language: Java;</p></li>
<li><p>Other requirements: Java ≥1.6, Apache Maven ≥3.0.4 (for developers);</p></li>
<li><p>License: GNU LGPL version 2.1 or later;</p></li>
<li><p>Any restrictions to use by non-academics: none.</p></li>
</ul></section><section id="ack1" class="ack"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Authors’ contributions</h2>
<p>SW developed the original MetFrag code under supervision of SN. CR rewrote MetFrag and performed all programming. CR and ES developed the additional features for MetFrag together and performed the data evaluation and interpretation. ES generated the test datasets and examples, CR and ES both prepared the manuscript. JH provided analytical advice on the concepts and integration of additional strategies; SN conceptualized MetFrag and provided advice on the informatics optimization. All authors read and approved the final manuscript.</p>
<section id="FPar2"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Acknowledgements</h3>
<p>The authors thank Heinz Singer, Michael Stravs, Birgit Beck and other members of the Environmental Chemistry Department at Eawag; Tobias Schulze, Martin Krauss and others at the Helmholtz Centre for Environmental Research (UFZ) involved in the acquisition of the Eawag and UFZ datasets, as well as Jennifer Schollée for performing the ChemAxon calculations, Martin Krauss for the unknown at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 199 data and Felicity Allen for her assistance with CFM-ID. CR acknowledges funding from DFG grant NE/1396/5-1, CR and ES acknowledge funding from EU FP7 project SOLUTIONS under Grant Agreement No. 603437.</p></section><section id="FPar1"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Competing interests</h3>
<p>The authors declare that they have no competing interests. The contributions of SW were independent of his employment at Bruker.</p></section></section><section id="App1" class="app"><section id="Sec24"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Additional files</h3>
